8.8469pt\hskip-8.8469pt\hbox{$\textstyle Q^{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{(f)}$}}$}\hskip-8.8469pt\hskip-220.50052pt\raisebox{10.72336pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-1.87527pt}{\resizebox{23367276}{407320}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1843165}{633951}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1843165}{633951}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2154901}{633951}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2656528}{633951}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-220.50052pt\hskip 8.8469pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 8.04301pt\hskip-8.04301pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle Q^{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{(f)}$}}$}\hskip-8.04301pt\hskip-220.50052pt\raisebox{10.14226pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-1.7462pt}{\resizebox{23367276}{379285}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1675683}{576346}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1675683}{576346}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1959092}{576346}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2415138}{576346}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-220.50052pt\hskip 8.04301pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 7.5997pt\hskip-7.5997pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle Q^{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{(f)}$}}$}\hskip-7.5997pt\hskip-220.50052pt\raisebox{9.43263pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-1.5921pt}{\resizebox{23367276}{345814}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1583323}{544579}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1583323}{544579}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1851111}{544579}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{2282021}{544579}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-220.50052pt\hskip 7.5997pt}}(x_{0},s+r_{1})}

(44)

\displaystyle=(s+r_{1}+r_{2})\frac{1-e^{-\sqrt{\tau_{d}(s+r_{1})}}}{s(s+r_{1}+r_{2})+r_{1}r_{2}e^{-\sqrt{\tau_{d}(s+r_{1})}}}.

(45)

4.2.1
Mean first passage time.

Equation (
45
) for
\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 6.1669pt\hskip-6.1669pt\hbox{$\displaystyle Q_{r}$}\hskip-6.1669pt\hskip-220.50052pt\raisebox{8.67775pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-1.44322pt}{\resizebox{23367276}{313478}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1284811}{441907}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1284811}{441907}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1502113}{441907}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1851781}{441907}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-220.50052pt\hskip 6.1669pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 6.1669pt\hskip-6.1669pt\hbox{$\textstyle Q_{r}$}\hskip-6.1669pt\hskip-220.50052pt\raisebox{8.67775pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-1.44322pt}{\resizebox{23367276}{313478}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1284811}{441907}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1284811}{441907}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1502113}{441907}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1851781}{441907}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-220.50052pt\hskip 6.1669pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 5.16396pt\hskip-5.16396pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle Q_{r}$}\hskip-5.16396pt\hskip-220.50052pt\raisebox{6.65779pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-1.01025pt}{\resizebox{23367276}{219435}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1075858}{370038}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1075858}{370038}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1257818}{370038}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1550619}{370038}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-220.50052pt\hskip 5.16396pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 4.72064pt\hskip-4.72064pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle Q_{r}$}\hskip-4.72064pt\hskip-215.5005pt\raisebox{5.31111pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.7216pt}{\resizebox{22711914}{156739}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{983500}{338272}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{983500}{338272}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1149841}{338272}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1417505}{338272}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-215.5005pt\hskip 4.72064pt}}(x_{0},s)
cannot be easily inverted to time domain, but it represents an excellent resource to compute some relevant physical properties in an exact way. Let us introduce the first passage density of the particle being absorbed by the target:

f_{\text{FPT}}(t;\tau_{d})=-\frac{\partial Q_{r}(x_{0},t)}{\partial t},

(46)

where we have introduced explicitly in the notation the parametric dependence on
\tau_{d}
. Therefrom, we derive the MFPT as the mean absorbing time,

\displaystyle T(r_{1},r_{2};\tau_{d})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mbox{d}t\,t\,f_{\text{FPT}}(t;\tau_{d})=\lim_{s\to 0}\mathchoice{\hbox{\hskip 6.1669pt\hskip-6.1669pt\hbox{$\displaystyle Q_{r}$}\hskip-6.1669pt\hskip-220.50052pt\raisebox{8.67775pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-1.44322pt}{\resizebox{23367276}{313478}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1284811}{441907}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1284811}{441907}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1502113}{441907}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1851781}{441907}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-220.50052pt\hskip 6.1669pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 6.1669pt\hskip-6.1669pt\hbox{$\textstyle Q_{r}$}\hskip-6.1669pt\hskip-220.50052pt\raisebox{8.67775pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-1.44322pt}{\resizebox{23367276}{313478}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1284811}{441907}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1284811}{441907}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1502113}{441907}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1851781}{441907}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-220.50052pt\hskip 6.1669pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 5.16396pt\hskip-5.16396pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle Q_{r}$}\hskip-5.16396pt\hskip-220.50052pt\raisebox{6.65779pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-1.01025pt}{\resizebox{23367276}{219435}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1075858}{370038}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1075858}{370038}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1257818}{370038}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1550619}{370038}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-220.50052pt\hskip 5.16396pt}}{\hbox{\hskip 4.72064pt\hskip-4.72064pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle Q_{r}$}\hskip-4.72064pt\hskip-215.5005pt\raisebox{5.31111pt}{\hbox{\raisebox{-0.7216pt}{\resizebox{22711914}{156739}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.94444pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{983500}{338272}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle\displaystyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{983500}{338272}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle\textstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1149841}{338272}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1417505}{338272}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.2mu\sim$}}}}}$}}}}}}\hskip-215.5005pt\hskip 4.72064pt}}(x_{0},s),

(47)

which is thus easily computable from equation (
45
),

\displaystyle T(r_{1},r_{2};\tau_{d})

\displaystyle=\left(e^{\sqrt{\tau_{d}r_{1}}}-1\right)\left(\frac{1}{r_{1}}+\frac{1}{r_{2}}\right).

(48)

Since we are interested in the dependence of the MFPT on the parameters controlling the typical duration of the reset events,
r_{1}
, and the refractory periods,
r_{2}
, we have introduced them explicitly in the notation.
As expected on a physical basis, two contributions appear in the MFPT coming from the two summands in the second parenthesis. The first one, which depends exclusively on
r_{1}
, corresponds to the instantaneous resetting without refractory period
[
1
,
2
]
. The second one stems from the refractory period that we have introduced after each resetting event.

Figure 4:
Mean first passage time
T(r_{1},r_{2})
as a function of the resetting rate
r_{1}
for fixed refractory rate
r_{2}
. An excellent agreement between simulations (symbols) and theory (solid lines) is found. The optimal resetting rate
r_{1}^{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{opt}$}}
, represented by five-pointed stars, monotonically increases with
r_{2}
—in the limit
r_{2}\to\infty
, the value
r_{1}^{(0)}=\left(2-W(-2e^{-2})\right)^{2}=2.53964
is reached. Inset: Optimal resetting rate as a function of the refractory period rate. We show the numerical solution of the implicit equation for
r_{1}^{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{opt}$}}
, as given by equation (
52
) (solid line), and the analytical approximation for small
r_{2}
, as given by equation (
54
) (dashed line).

Despite its simple functional form, equation (
48
) exhibits atypical and rich behaviour. We are interested in how the MFPT changes as a function of
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
—clearly, it monotonically increases with
\tau_{d}
, that is, it increases with the distance
|x_{t}-x_{0}|
and the inverse of diffusion coefficient
D
. With this knowledge, we are able to write equation (
48
) as a function which only depends on
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
introducing dimensionless parameters
r_{1}=r_{1}^{*}/\tau_{d}
,
r_{2}=r_{2}^{*}/\tau_{d}
and
T=T^{*}\tau_{d}
. Therefore, the MFPT is

T(r_{1},r_{2})=\left(e^{\sqrt{r_{1}}}-1\right)\left(\frac{1}{r_{1}}+\frac{1}{r_{2}}\right),

(49)

where we have dropped the asterisk in order not to clutter the formulae. This result was also obtained in an apparently different context
[
68
]
, when considering and intermittent V-shaped potential that was randomly switched on and off with different rates. Reasonably, in the limit of the stiffness of the potential going to infinity, the stochastic resetting with refractory periods is recovered.

4.2.2
Optimal resetting rate
r_{1}^{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{opt}$}}(r_{2})

Let us first look for the minimum of
T(r_{1},r_{2})
as a function of
r_{2}
for fixed
r_{1}
. We clearly get that
r_{2}\to\infty
for the best choice, reobtaining the MFPT in the absence of refractory period
[
1
,
2
]
,

\lim_{r_{2}\to\infty}T(r_{1},r_{2})=\frac{e^{\sqrt{r_{1}}}-1}{r_{1}}.

(50)

As a function of
r_{1}
, the minimum of
T(r_{1},r_{2}\to\infty)
is reached at
r_{1}=r_{1}^{(0)}=\left(2+W(-2e^{-2})\right)^{2}
, where
W(x)
corresponds to the Lambert
W
function, which is the inverse function of equation
f(x)=xe^{x}
, i.e. the solution of
W(x)\exp[W(x)]=x

[
1
,
2
]
.

Now we focus on looking for the minimum of
T(r_{1},r_{2})
as a function of
r_{1}
for fixed
r_{2}
. In figure
4
, we show a couple of instances of
T(r_{1},r_{2})
as a function of
r_{1}
for
r_{2}\in\{1,5\}
, finding an excellent agreement between theory and simulations. The figure highlights the existence of a certain optimal curve
r_{1}^{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{opt}$}}=r_{1}^{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{opt}$}}(r_{2})
, which is obtained by solving

0=\left.\frac{\partial T(r_{1},r_{2})}{\partial r_{1}}\right|_{r_{1}^{\mbox{\tiny$\text{opt}$}}}=\left.\frac{2r_{2}\left(1-e^{\sqrt{r_{1}}}\right)+\sqrt{r_{1}}e^{\sqrt{r_{1}}}\left(r_{1}+r_{2}\right)}{2r_{1}^{2}r_{2}}\right|_{r_{1}^{\mbox{\tiny$\text{opt}$}}}.

(51)

Then,
r_{1}^{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{opt}$}}
is implicitly given as a function of
r_{2}
by

2r_{2}\left(1-e^{\sqrt{r_{1}^{\mbox{\tiny$\text{opt}$}}}}\right)+\sqrt{r_{1}^{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{opt}$}}}e^{\sqrt{r_{1}^{\mbox{\tiny$\text{opt}$}}}}\left(r_{1}^{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{opt}$}}+r_{2}\right)=0.

(52)

Now we analyse the limiting behaviour of
r_{1}^{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{opt}$}}
for both
r_{2}\to\infty
(no refractory period) and
r_{2}\to 0
(infinite refractory period). On the one hand, for
r_{2}\to\infty
, we obtain that the limiting value
\lim_{r_{2}\to\infty}r_{1}^{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{opt}$}}(r_{2})
is determined by

\lim_{r_{2}\to\infty}2\left[r_{1}^{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{opt}$}}(r_{2})\right]^{-1/2}\left[1-e^{-\sqrt{r_{1}^{\mbox{\tiny$\text{opt}$}}(r_{2})}}\right]=1,

(53)

i.e.
\lim_{r_{2}\to\infty}r_{1}^{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{opt}$}}(r_{2})=r_{1}^{(0)}
—which is consistent with the optimal resetting strategy without refractory period previously introduced. On the other hand, it is clear from equation (
52
) that
\lim_{r_{2}\to 0}r_{1}^{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{opt}$}}(r_{2})=0
, which is logical from a physical point of view: for infinite refractory period, the best strategy for the MFPT is to avoid resetting. A dominant balance argument shows that
r_{1}^{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{opt}$}}\sim r_{2}
in this limit.

In order to further investigate the dependence of
r_{1}^{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{opt}$}}
on
r_{2}
for long refractory periods (small
r_{2}
), it is handy to expand
r_{1}^{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{opt}$}}
in a power series of
\sqrt{r_{2}}
.
Substituting this expansion into equation (
52
), one gets after a little bit of algebra

r_{1}^{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{opt}$}}=r_{2}-r_{2}^{3/2}+\frac{5}{6}r_{2}^{2}+O\left(r_{2}^{5/2}\right).

(54)

A comparison between the expansion (
54
) and the numerical estimate for
r_{1}^{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{opt}$}}
is shown in the inset of figure
4
.

Our result shows that there appears a “resonance” phenomenon, which optimises the MFPT—making it minimum—for a resetting rate that is linked with the refractory period rate. When the resetting point
x_{0}
and the target
x_{t}
are close, in the sense that
r_{2}\tau_{d}\ll 1
,
r_{1}^{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{opt}$}}\simeq r_{2}
. As
r_{2}
is increased,
r_{1}^{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{opt}$}}
consequently increases but it asymptotically saturates for large enough values of
r_{2}
: for
r_{2}\tau_{d}\gg 1
, the optimum MFPT asymptotically tends to its limiting value
r_{1}^{(0)}
corresponding to stochastic resetting without refractory periods.

5
Conclusions

In this work, we have carried out a thorough analysis of the effects of introducing a time cost to stochastic resets in a one-dimensional Brownian searcher. First, we have exploited a pathway formulation to derive general results. This puts forward an alternative, appealing from a physical point of view, methodology to address the study of intermittent dynamics. Second, we have particularised the results for the relevant case of Poissonian resetting events and refractory periods. Therein, not only have we obtained the non-equilibrium stationary state, but also a detailed solution of the transient dynamics in the time domain. Finally, we have studied in depth the single-target search problem with refractory period. Specifically, we have investigated the optimal strategy for resetting, in the sense of minimising the mean first passage time to the target, finding that the optimal resetting rate depends on the typical duration of the refractory phase.

From a physical perspective, the final result on the optimal resetting rate to find a target is especially interesting. The dependence of the optimal resetting rate on the refractory period is somehow counterintuitive, since the duration of the time intervals between resetting events and the duration of the refractory phase are independent in our model. The optimal strategy entails a non-trivial, resonance-like behaviour in which the optimal resetting rate equals the inverse of the characteristic time of the refractory period. This phenomenon is reminiscent of resonant activation
[
69
,
70
]
where, when considering the escape problem in a two-well potential mediated by a fluctuating barrier, an optimal fluctuation rate that minimises the escape time emerges. Indeed, our resetting setup with refractory periods can be thought of as a fluctuating potential that switches between a totally confining potential trap and zero. Just recently, the potential connection between optimal resetting and resonant activation has started to be explored
[
71
]
, which provides an interesting perspective for further research.

Data availability

The codes employed for generating the data that support the findings of this study, together with the Mathematica notebooks employed for producing the figures presented in the paper, are openly available in the
GitHub page
of University of Sevilla’s FINE research group.

Appendix A
Asymptotic analysis of the PDF for the propagation phase in the long-time limit

In this appendix, we derive an approximate explicit expression for the different integrals appearing in equation (
33
). For the sake of compactness, in the remaining of this appendix, we take
x_{0}=0
. The general result can be reobtained at the end with the substitution
x\to x-x_{0}
.

We start by focusing on the first integral term in equation (
33
),

\displaystyle I_{1}=\int_{0}^{1}\,\mbox{d}\omega\,\omega^{-1/2}\,e^{-r_{1}t\,[\omega+x^{2}/(4D\omega r_{1}t^{2})]}=\int_{0}^{1}\,\mbox{d}\omega\,\omega^{-1/2}\,e^{-\omega t^{*}-\frac{{x^{*}}^{2}}{\omega t^{*}}},

(55)

where we have introduced the following dimensionless variables
t^{*}=r_{1}t
and
x^{*}=x/\sqrt{4Dr_{1}^{-1}}
. In the following, we drop the asterisks for the sake of a clearer notation. For long times, Laplace’s method tells us that the integral is dominated by the maximum of the exponent, i.e. the minimum of
\phi_{1}(\omega)=\omega t+x^{2}/(\omega t)
inside the integration interval
(0,1)
. Since the function
\phi_{1}(\omega)
has a relative minimum at
\omega_{0}=|x|/t
, how to estimate the integral depends on the value of
\omega_{0}
, specifically on whether
\omega_{0}
is larger or smaller than unity. Then, it is handy to introduce the change of variable
\omega=\omega_{0}\nu
,

I_{1}=\omega_{0}^{1/2}\int_{0}^{1/\omega_{0}}\,\mbox{d}\nu\,\nu^{-1/2}\,e^{-\omega_{0}t\,\psi(\nu)},

(56)

where we we have introduced
\psi(\nu)=\nu+1/\nu
, which attains its relative minimum at
\nu_{0}=1
. Now we asymptotically estimate
I_{1}
in the long-time limit
t\gg 1
, with
\omega_{0}=\mathcal{O}(1)
, i.e.
x=\mathcal{O}(t)
. We must discriminate between different cases:

1.

The relative minimum of
\psi(\nu)
at
\nu=1
lies inside the interval
(0,1/\omega_{0})
, i.e.
\omega_{0}<1
or
|x|<t
, and, in addition, it is far enough from the upper limit, in a sense that is clarified below.

The idea of Laplace’s method is to expand
\psi(\nu)
around the relative minimum at
\nu=1
,

\omega_{0}t\psi(\nu)\sim 2\omega_{0}t+\omega_{0}t(\nu-1)^{2},

(57)

which leads to a Gaussian centred at
\nu=1
and very small width, proportional to
(\omega_{0}t)^{-1/2}\ll 1
4
4

4

Note that the following term in the Taylor series of
\omega_{0}t\psi(\nu)
is
-\omega_{0}t(\nu-1)^{3}
, which is negligible against the retained quadratic term
\omega_{0}t(\nu-1)^{2}
where the Gaussian contributes to the integral, i.e. for
\nu-1=O(\omega_{0}t)^{-1/2}
.
. Therefore, the dominant behaviour of the integral comes from a narrow interval around
\nu=1
. Here, we consider that
\omega_{0}
is such that the whole Gaussian belongs in the integration interval
(0,1/\omega_{0})
, i.e. the integral is dominated by the contribution from
(1-\varepsilon,1+\varepsilon)
, with
\varepsilon\ll 1
, because we can choose
\varepsilon
such that

\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{in}$}}\equiv 1/\omega_{0}-1>\varepsilon\gg(\omega_{0}t)^{-1/2}.

(58)

With this line of reasoning,

I_{1}\sim I^{(i)}_{1}=\omega_{0}^{1/2}e^{-2\omega_{0}t}\int_{1-\varepsilon}^{1+\varepsilon}\,\mbox{d}\nu\,e^{-\omega_{0}t\,(\nu-1)^{2}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}e^{-2\omega_{0}t}\int_{-\varepsilon\sqrt{\omega_{0}t}}^{+\varepsilon\sqrt{\omega_{0}t}}\,\mbox{d}z\,e^{-z^{2}}\sim\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{t}}e^{-2|x|},

(59)

where condition (
58
) allows for the integration limits in the last integral be extended to
\pm\infty
. Note that the obtained expression corresponds to the first case in equation (
36
) if we reintroduce dimensions, i.e. the NESS behaviour (
32a
).

2.

The relative minimum of
\psi(\nu)
at
\nu=1
lies outside the interval
(0,1/\omega_{0})
, i.e.
\omega_{0}>1
or
|x|>t
and, in addition, it is far enough from the upper limit, in a sense that is also clarified below.

In this case, the minimum of
\psi(\nu)
within the interval occurs at the upper limit
1/\omega_{0}
. Hence, Laplace’s method tells us to expand
\psi(\nu)
around
\nu=1/\omega_{0}
,
\psi(\nu)\simeq\omega_{0}+1/\omega_{0}+(1-\omega_{0}^{2})(\nu-1/\omega_{0})+\omega_{0}^{3}(\nu-1/\omega_{0})^{2}
and restrict the integral to a narrow interval
(1/\omega_{0}-\epsilon,1/\omega_{0})
, with
\varepsilon\ll 1
. The quadratic term is negligible against the linear one if

\frac{\omega_{0}^{2}-1}{\omega_{0}^{3}}\gg\varepsilon.

(60)

Assuming this “far enough” condition holds, we have

\displaystyle I_{1}\sim I^{(ii)}_{1}

\displaystyle=\omega_{0}\,e^{-t(1+\omega_{0}^{2})}\int_{1/\omega_{0}-\varepsilon}^{1/\omega_{0}}\,\mbox{d}\nu\,e^{\omega_{0}t\,\left(\omega_{0}^{2}-1\right)\left(\nu-1/\omega_{0}\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-t(1+\omega_{0}^{2})}}{t\left(\omega_{0}^{2}-1\right)}\int_{-\omega_{0}t\left(\omega_{0}^{2}-1\right)\varepsilon}^{0}\,\mbox{d}z\,e^{z}\sim{\frac{e^{-t(1+x^{2}/t^{2})}}{t\left(\frac{x^{2}}{t^{2}}-1\right)},}

(61)

provided that the extension of the lower limit to
-\infty
can be justified, i.e. we can choose
\varepsilon
such that

\omega_{0}t\left(\omega_{0}^{2}-1\right)\varepsilon\gg 1.

(62)

Conditions (
60
) and (
62
) can be fulfilled without problems when
\omega_{0}-1=O(1)
, since they tell us that we have to choose
\varepsilon
small but much larger than
(\omega_{0}t)^{-1}\ll 1
. As
\omega_{0}
approaches unity,
1/\omega_{0}=1-\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}
, with
\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}\ll 1
, conditions (
60
) and (
62
) entail that

\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}\gg\varepsilon,\quad\omega_{0}t\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}\varepsilon\gg 1\implies\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}\gg(\omega_{0}t)^{-1/2}.

(63)

This “far enough” condition makes sense: it is telling us that the separation of the upper limit from unity (the position of the relative minimum of
\psi
) must be much larger than the width of the Gaussian, analogously to condition (
58
).

Equation (
61
) has the same dominant contribution in the exponent that the non-resetting term
p^{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{(f)}$}}(x,t|x_{0})
. However, the tails of the PDF are dominated by the non-resetting term, as expressed by equation (
36
), since the coefficient of equation (
61
),
(x^{2}/t)^{-1}
, is subdominant compared to that in
p^{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{(f)}$}}
,
t^{-1/2}
, for
x/t=O(1)
.

3.

The relative minimum of
\psi(\nu)
at
\nu=1
lies inside the interval
(0,1/\omega_{0})
, i.e.
\omega_{0}<1
or
|x|<t
, but it is close to the upper limit,
1/\omega_{0}=1+\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{in}$}}
with
\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{in}$}}
not fulfilling condition (
58
), i.e.
\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{in}$}}=O(\omega_{0}t)^{-1/2}
. Herein, we have

\displaystyle I_{1}\sim I^{(iii)}_{1}

\displaystyle=\omega_{0}^{1/2}e^{-2\omega_{0}t}\int_{1-\varepsilon}^{1+\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{in}$}}}\,\mbox{d}\nu\,e^{-\omega_{0}t\,(\nu-1)^{2}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}e^{-2\omega_{0}t}\int_{-\varepsilon\sqrt{\omega_{0}t}}^{+\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{in}$}}\sqrt{\omega_{0}t}}\,\mbox{d}z\,e^{-z^{2}}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{t}}\mbox{erfc}\left(\frac{|x|-t}{\sqrt{|x|}}\right)e^{-2|x|},

(64)

where we have considered that
\varepsilon
can always be choosen such that
\varepsilon\sqrt{\omega_{0}t}\gg 1
. Note that this expression asymptotically converges to
I^{(i)}_{1}
in equation (
59
) when the limit
\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{in}$}}\sqrt{\omega_{0}t}\gg 1
is considered. Thus, this expression may be used to approximate
I_{1}
for
\omega_{0}<1
, i.e.
|x|<t
, regardless of the value of
\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{in}$}}
.

4.

The relative minimum of
\psi(\nu)
at
\nu=1
lies outside the interval
(0,1/\omega_{0})
, i.e.
\omega_{0}>1
or
|x|>t
but it is close to the upper limit, i.e.
1/\omega_{0}=1-\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}
with
\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}
not fulfilling condition (
63
), i.e.
\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}=O(\omega_{0}t)^{-1/2}
.

Following the general idea of the Laplace method, we expand
\psi(\nu)
around
1/\omega_{0}=1-\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}
in a narrow interval
(1-\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}-\varepsilon,1-\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}})
. In (ii),
\varepsilon\ll\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}
, but here we consider that
\varepsilon
is at least
O(\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}})
. Therefore, we get

\omega_{0}t\psi(\nu)\simeq\omega_{0}t\left[2+\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}^{2}+2\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}(\nu-1+\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}})+(\nu-1+\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}})^{2}\right],

(65)

neglecting
O(\omega_{0}t\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}^{3})
,
O(\omega_{0}t\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}^{2}\varepsilon)
,
O(\omega_{0}t\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}\varepsilon^{2})
, and
O(\omega_{0}t\varepsilon^{3})
terms.
5
5

5

Recalling that
\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}=O(\omega_{0}t)^{-1/2}
, we have, on the one hand,
O(\omega_{0}t\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}^{3})=O(\omega_{0}t)^{-1/2}\ll 1
,
O(\omega_{0}t\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}^{2}\varepsilon)=O(\varepsilon)\ll 1
. On the other hand, both
O(\omega_{0}t\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}\varepsilon^{2})=O((\omega_{0}t)^{1/2}\varepsilon^{2})
and
O(\omega_{0}t\varepsilon^{3})
must be much smaller than unity, so
\varepsilon\ll(\omega_{0}t)^{-1/3}
.
Introducing this expansion, we obtain

I_{1}\sim\omega_{0}\,e^{-(2+\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}^{2})\omega_{0}t}\int_{1-\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}-\varepsilon}^{1-\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}}\,d\nu\,e^{-\omega_{0}t\left[2\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}(\nu-1+\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}})+(\nu-1+\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}})^{2}\right]}.

(66)

The change of variables
2\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}\omega_{0}t(\nu-1+\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}})=z
allows us to write

I_{1}\sim\frac{1}{2\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}t}\,e^{-(2+\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}^{2})\omega_{0}t}\int_{-2\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}\omega_{0}t\varepsilon}^{0}\,\mbox{d}z\,e^{z-z^{2}/(4\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}^{2}\omega_{0}t)}.

(67)

Now we choose
\varepsilon
such that
\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}\omega_{0}t\varepsilon\gg 1
, which makes it possible to extend the lower limit of the integral to
-\infty
, similarly to the other cases we have analysed.
6
6

6

Taking into account, once again, that
\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}=O(\omega_{0}t)^{-1/2}
, this implies that
\varepsilon\gg(\omega_{0}t)^{-1/2}
. So our small parameter
\varepsilon
must verify
(\omega_{0}t)^{-1/3}\gg\varepsilon\gg(\omega_{0}t)^{-1/2}
, e.g. we may take
\varepsilon\propto(\omega_{0}t)^{-2/5}
.
Finally, the explicit approximation we get is

\displaystyle I_{1}\sim I^{(iv)}_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}t}\,e^{-(2+\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}^{2})\omega_{0}t}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\,\mbox{d}z\,e^{z-z^{2}/(4\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}^{2}\omega_{0}t)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}t}\,e^{-(2+\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}^{2})\omega_{0}t}\,\sqrt{\pi\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}^{2}\omega_{0}t}\,e^{\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}^{2}\omega_{0}t}\mbox{erfc}\left(\sqrt{\delta_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{out}$}}^{2}\omega_{0}t}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\pi|x|}}{2t}\,e^{-2|x|}\mbox{erfc}\left(\frac{|x|-t}{\sqrt{|x|}}\right).

(68)

The other integral term involved in equation (
33
) has a simpler analysis. We employ again dimensionless variables, but now those stemming from the natural units evidenced by
\Phi_{2}
, i.e.
x^{*}=x/\sqrt{4Dr_{2}^{-1}}
and
t^{*}=r_{2}t
—and drop the asterisks once more. The exponent
\Phi_{2}(\omega;y)
is a monotonically increasing function of
\omega
. Thus, the local maximum of the exponent within the integration interval occurs for all cases at the upper limit. Similarly to the case (ii) for
I_{1}
, direct application of Laplace’s method gives

I_{2}=\int_{0}^{1}\,\mbox{d}\omega\,\omega^{-1/2}\,e^{\omega t-\frac{x^{2}}{\omega t}}\sim\frac{e^{t(1-x^{2}/t^{2})}}{t(1+x^{2}/t^{2})}=\left(x^{2}/t+t\right)^{-1}e^{t(1-x^{2}/t^{2})}.

(69)

In figure
3
, the evaluation of the theoretical prediction (
33
) is performed by computing the approximated expressions in this appendix for
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
.
7
7

7

We recall that those expressions depend on dimensionless magnitudes that involve
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
, respectively.
For the inner region,
I_{1}^{(iii)}
is used, while
I_{1}^{(iv)}
and
I_{1}^{(ii)}
are used for the outer region. The change between
I_{1}^{(iv)}
and
I_{1}^{(ii)}
is made at the crossing point
x_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\mbox{cross}$}}>0
where
I_{1}^{(iv)}=I_{1}^{(ii)}
, which has been numerically obtained.
Hence, the plotted line stands for:

p^{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{(p)}$}}(x,t|x_{0})\sim\left\{\begin{aligned} p^{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{(f)}$}}(x,t|x_{0})+I_{1}^{(iii)}-I_{2}&,\quad|x|<t,\\
p^{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{(f)}$}}(x,t|x_{0})+I_{1}^{(iv)}-I_{2}&,\quad t<|x|<x_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\mbox{cross}$}},\\
p^{\mbox{\scriptsize$\text{(f)}$}}(x,t|x_{0})+I_{1}^{(ii)}-I_{2}&,\quad x_{\mbox{\scriptsize$\mbox{cross}$}}<|x|.\end{aligned}\right.

(70)
